[12/9/2025 3:17 PM] ascpete_25859
Several years ago, as a hobby beginner 'Kitchen Culture Kits' and Microclone websites provided some basic info, Protocols and Materials.
Also for beginners an inexpensive room HEPA Air Purifier used in an isolated / separated 'Clean Space' (closet, bathroom, etc?) can eliminate the need for the added expense of 'Laminar Flow Hoods'.

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1447005068623876187/1447955266971631737/image.png?ex=6af3276d&is=6af1d5ed&hm=8e49a14e894c8ccbdc250dbf66c236d6e2afa1a4c08de4949874c3043b4123bc&

[12/9/2025 6:43 PM] Deleted User
oh okay


[12/9/2025 6:44 PM] zap233
But they're at Walmart and target and easy to cut


[12/9/2025 6:44 PM] zap233
I've used them for filament dry boxes before


[12/9/2025 6:45 PM] zap233
3 things- it's a more managable size and easier to transport than the vinyl plastic ones.  It hasn't clouded up from alcohol, and it doesn't have the drawback of flexible sides causing air movement


[12/9/2025 6:47 PM] zap233
I literally went from over 50% contamination rates to under 10% around the time I switched


[12/9/2025 6:51 PM] zap233
I will say it's still worth building a laminar flow unit, but a SAB is handy as something portable and compact they're just a bit of a pain to work with.


[12/9/2025 7:57 PM] ascpete_25859
Its much simpler to get an adequately sized room HEPA Air Purifier (run for several hours) plus a $6.00 plastic tub on its side (just in case), no flow hood required. 😉


[12/9/2025 8:16 PM] zap233
For my sake it was worth while.  (I already run 2 genuine HEPA filters in my home, one in that room)


[12/9/2025 8:17 PM] zap233
Would probably be beneficial if I were doing a clean room type set up though

[12/9/2025 9:07 PM] zap233
Plants in TC need some light, it doesn't have to be much.  You don't want them to cook from greenhouse effect in direct sun, and you don't want them completely in the dark because then you run into certain weird issues like hyperhydricidity.


[12/9/2025 9:08 PM] Deleted User
yeah I'm just talking about the room just being illuminated by some amount of indirect light, no direct sun or anything


[12/9/2025 9:09 PM] Deleted User
my main question is whether I need to cover the rack and exclusively give it the artificial light in a controlled way


[12/9/2025 9:10 PM] Deleted User
or is it fine if there is some fluctuation/randomness


[12/9/2025 9:15 PM] zap233
Randomness should be fine

[12/9/2025 9:33 PM] ascpete_25859
You should get an inexpensive Lux meter and simply check it.
Most TC Protocols include actual photo-periods (PP) and light intensity (LUX, PAR/PPFD, or FC)

Lux measurements can be used for any inexpensive household 4000K - 5500K LED light sources

Foot Candles (FC) to Lux: FC x 10.8 = Lux
300 FC x 10.8 = 3240 Lux

PPFD to Lux: PPFD / 0.014 = Lux
50 PPFD / 0.014 = 3571 Lux

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1447005068623876187/1448049997546524734/image.webp?ex=6af2d6e6&is=6af18566&hm=933a0dcdb9681b941b2726799b7048fd4b1703a1e57d29e207860e873d5e476c&

[12/13/2025 8:57 PM] kangzo
Will do, In general i have been pretty content with the 100+ eur stuff i've bought from China. Most stuff really is just basic components that you can reliably use. You just have to do some basic research and check for feedback from other people who buy stuff from there. and realise that calibration can be a little off with "precision" equipment. It will still do the job 8 out of 10 times flawlessly.

[12/14/2025 11:06 PM] joshakazam
Anyone have a good video/resource on how to make your own still air box? Also is there a cheaper pressure cooker/instapot that people have found and had success with? 600+ dollars total is a lot to drop on a new hobby and the pressure cooker is the largest expense..


[12/15/2025 12:32 AM] plantsinjars
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g8pso7WOKtQ

{Embed}
Von Malegowski
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g8pso7WOKtQ
How to Make and Use a Still Air Box
This video is about the process of making and using a still air box for growing mushrooms. It is made from a large plastic container. Two armholes are melted into the container with a coffee can to make perfect holes. The edges of the holes are made smooth with some sandpaper. The second half of the video explains some good practices for using a...
https://images-ext-1.discordapp.net/external/c96XspUhotkauhXBhPprWj8MMAkQkAwW0EsZPjNsjDc/https/i.ytimg.com/vi/g8pso7WOKtQ/maxresdefault.jpg


[12/15/2025 12:32 AM] plantsinjars
good tutorial


[12/15/2025 12:33 AM] plantsinjars
you can also add sleeves like the one I used in the video if you wanted to make it even a little bit better


[12/15/2025 12:34 AM] plantsinjars
as for the pressure cooker, you can use an instapot (as long as it has a slow cook setting) or tbh you might want to check FB marketplace. I agree the pressure cooker is expensive. They were under $100 when I first got started doing tc and I swear, every time I link them under the vid I notice they have increased in price

[12/18/2025 11:40 AM] ebo1744
The whole InstantPot discussion in complicated.  There are different models, and they each have their capabilities.  The Duo (which I own) can only do a little over 11 PSI, which will get up to the published pressure/temperature recommended in food safety up to maybe 2,000' (600m) elevation (I'm at 130'', or 40m elevation, so it fully works for me).  The InstantPot-Max can do something like 15 PSI and has been tested up to 5,000' (1,500m).  I'll see if I can find the video of a researcher testing a Max.  So, if you are down near the coast, a Duo should work for you.  If you are up in the Alps, the Max might or might not work for you...  Also, the whole PSI elevation thing comes from food-safety recommendations to ensure botulism kill-off in canning.  There were a fair bit of deaths back in the late 1800's and early 1900's, and I think the pressure recommendations were established during the Great Depression (1930's).  If you get some TC media contaminated, it is NOT likely going to KILL YOU or your family.  That is the whole basis for the PSI canning recommendations.  So like much of the stuff discussed here, give it a try, see what works, tell us so that we can dial in our own trials, and best of luck!

[12/20/2025 11:03 AM] kindred.0833
Dissolving PGR is not as scary as it seems (still have be safe etc). Here's how to make 1M NaOH solution https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p1iiwXVf_Po&t=8s.  The stock solution can be stored so you don't have to make it up each time. Then it's simply just a matter of diluting your PGR with some of the NaOH solution, then topping up with water to whatever concentration you need: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XI3pH_X_4TI .  Simplifying of course, but it's really not that hard. You can do it!

Not sure about PGR stores in Singapore, but food grade 100% NaOH is usually also sold at hobby brewing stores. I don't brew anything lol - it's the easiest place for me to grab it.

Yeah they're crossing them with the resistant Chinese chestnut (since the blight is native to asia) and then backcrossing with American chestnut so they're like 95% American but resistant still. There's also a long standing hunt for pure resistant american chestnut specimens and a breeding program for those too, but with much less success so far. [12/21/2025 10:45 PM] ebo1744
I had not heard about it effecting the sassafras.  Deep sadness.


[12/21/2025 10:48 PM] ebo1744
This is complicated.  There is one group that has been hybridizing them with the Chinese Chestnuts, and then back crossing to American Chestnuts to get just the genes they need.  There is another group that tangentially spliced the resistance gene from wheat into American Chestnut and grew them for a LONG time (15 to 20 years), and only then found that the strain they choose and grew out was actually mislabeled and did NOT contain  what they were reporting that it contained...

{Reactions}
Glorb (2)

[12/21/2025 10:50 PM] ebo1744
There are also 2 or 3 different diseases that are hammering the American Chestnuts, the first is the infamous blight, and the second is a root rot that will get it even if it can survive the blight.  The endophyte we are playing with is efficacious against the rot, but not the blight...

[12/24/2025 7:49 PM] zap233
Check the manual of your pressure cooker, most say that you can't use them with glass tops.  I think the main concern is cracking the glass due to the weight and thermal differential.


[12/24/2025 7:59 PM] chefdecuisinart
Yeah, not all glass tops are the same thickness.


[12/24/2025 8:00 PM] chefdecuisinart
KitchenAid glass tops say they're safe as long as you use the large double size burner. I've had no issues with mine.


[12/24/2025 8:17 PM] noneya123456
Burner size and pot must match. Otherwise the heat distribution will be off and can cause glass to crack. Also if bottom is not completely flat with complete contact the suction created can crack glass. If it was mine I’d risk it, but I rent in a “luxury” high rise and know they’d hit me with like a $2k bill if I crack it, so for me the $700 or so for vertical autoclave is just going to be buying insurance on not risking paying $2k for a broke glass stovetop

Todos los macros, micros, hormonas y ppm (si usas) , agar se agregan a tu solución madre. Luego lo dosificas en tus recipientes. Posteriormente lo esterilizas a 25 psi a 121 grados Celsius por 30 minutos.


[12/27/2025 10:55 PM] charly9
Si vas añadir antibióticos en polvo, si se agregan después de esterilizar, ya que la esterilización inhibe el efecto del antibiótico

[1/2/2026 6:31 PM] juzzy__
so the reason I say that. If flood acid batteries (cheaper RV batteries) get below 50% the voltage drops dramatically. then you have heat in the motors, and if they drop below like 25% you'll risk gassing out the batteries


[1/2/2026 6:32 PM] chefdecuisinart
Nah, I'm going to use Lithium phosphate


[1/2/2026 6:32 PM] juzzy__
LiFePO4 batteries are more expensive, if they drop too low they will stop receiveing a charge all together

hi, is that roof access on the ceiling ? I worked in a uB lab with old vents, we got some huge and random contaminations . In the end we found that when someone opened lab room door (when we had  petri dishes on the table etc) the airflow got reverced and air from the "attic" went to the room. We neded to do upgrate the vents with fans to force the air flow.

[1/8/2026 2:20 PM] trashpanda5911

[1/8/2026 2:27 PM] alexrandomkat
eh I'm not too sure either, I just read a review on areole activation and saw they do not rip out the spines (despite them also acknowledging they can be hard to sterilize). If you do shoot multiplication (i.e. only supply cytokinin) it's basically applying pupping hormone to "reactivate" the dormant meristem to pup. Otherwise you can make callus and have it form new meristems all over again (at least is my rough understanding), but that takes at least two steps with a callus-inducing media and shoot-inducing media.

also I know a few cacti species have meristems buried deeper under the areole than most, but idk if this applies to trichocereus.

If I were to rip out the spines, I'd probably do it after letting them sit in bleach. Cacti can only stand the harsh 20 min in 20% bleach routine bc of their thick cuticle.


[1/8/2026 2:36 PM] alexrandomkat
though guessing from the observation of pups occasionally busting open plants and also being annoying to cut away (my graft stock keeps trying to pup from a cut areole!) I'm gonna guess trichocereus do have deeper meristems...

[1/19/2026 9:36 PM] zap233
Um


[1/19/2026 9:36 PM] zap233
So it's boiling the water after?


[1/19/2026 9:36 PM] zap233
That requires power.


[1/19/2026 9:36 PM] zap233
Or like a solar still.


[1/19/2026 9:37 PM] grumpyalison
No it just removes stuff via filtration vs collecting steam etc. 
I’ve tested it with a tds meter and it’s the same as storebought distilled on that front


[1/19/2026 9:38 PM] zap233
Okay in that case it is not distillation.


[1/19/2026 9:39 PM] zap233
The process of distillation requires a phase change from liquid to gas and back to liquid, leaving the dissolved solids behind.


[1/19/2026 9:40 PM] zap233
RO water is usually close to equivalent in TDS, although the process used is different.


[1/19/2026 9:42 PM] grumpyalison
Fair. In reef tank space we usually just call it all distilled water because lazy 😂

[1/20/2026 12:25 AM] melonpultfrompvz
this distiller cost me 64.57 USD to buy, and the power consumption is about 50-60% of an air conditioner at most


[1/20/2026 12:26 AM] melonpultfrompvz
distilled bottled water is like 1.10 USD here


[1/20/2026 12:27 AM] melonpultfrompvz
it'll pay for itself after making 60L of distilled water excluding the electricity cost


[1/20/2026 12:30 AM] melonpultfrompvz
the electricity cost for 45 kWh is like 3.71 USD at most


[1/20/2026 12:33 AM] melonpultfrompvz
so (64.57+3.71)/1.10 = ~62


[1/20/2026 12:33 AM] melonpultfrompvz
it's gonna pay for itself after 62 litres of water


[1/20/2026 12:37 AM] melonpultfrompvz
[it shuts off the heating after the water reaches a certain temperature, so I'm actually probably overestimating the electricity usage]


[1/20/2026 12:43 AM] melonpultfrompvz
if I use even just 2 litres of distilled water daily on average, 31 days is all it's going to take for the price to justify itself


[1/20/2026 12:55 AM] zap233
For my usage I'm calculating around $592.11 per year in electrical costs using 5 gallons per week at a 16 cent per kWh rate locally.  

Compared to RO, which is generating around 4 gallons of waste water for every 1 gallon purified, at 5gal/week, and a local rate of $5.48 per ccf(100 cubic feet), I'm paying about $47.75.


[1/20/2026 12:56 AM] zap233
So while it may be cheaper than buying it pre-bottled, switching to an RO unit will likely save you money in the long term.


[1/20/2026 12:57 AM] zap233
This is based on the specs of the unit above, using 750w for 1 liter per hour.

[1/28/2026 7:52 PM] zap233

[1/28/2026 7:55 PM] zap233

[2/1/2026 8:46 PM] zap233
For my workflow with a 22 quart pressure cooker, sterilizing the media in the flasks after pouring, I can do about 2L in about 5-6 hours on a weekend afternoon.  Depending on the container, I use between 25-35mL each, which nets me about 70 flasks to work with over the next few weeks.  (I usually don't do much more than a dozen per session)

[2/12/2026 6:16 PM] tommy_44922
Hi guys, i'm new to tissue culture, i have a question about safety when working under the still airbox: so, everything that goes inside the sab needs to be sprayed with 70% alcohl, but if i use a glass beed sterilizer isn't it dangerous to spray alcohl inside the box as the sterilizer can reach up to 200 celsius (392 farenheit) ?
Also do i spray the outside of the sterilizer before putting it in the box or when it's already inside the box? Probably the last option would lead to more vapour of alcohl forming in the box and an higher probability of EXPLOSION isn't it?


[2/12/2026 6:34 PM] chefdecuisinart
IPA's ignition temperature is like 400C/750F


[2/12/2026 6:37 PM] chefdecuisinart
In theory, your glass bead sterilizer shouldn't have anything sparking in it. I used a vapor filled SAB for a long time with a cheap nail tech bead sterilizer, never started a fire.


[2/12/2026 6:47 PM] tommy_44922
I see, thanks you.
But just in case i think i'm going to: firstly put everything inside the SAB and spray it, then switch on the sterilizer and wait 10-15 minutes for the alcohl to evaporate and for the nail sterilizer to become hot, even though some vapour will probably remain as it can only escape through the sleeves of the SAB

[2/12/2026 8:58 PM] erwin_myco
Having a bead steriliser inside the still air box will ruin the point though, that will create moving air

[4/13/2026 6:55 PM] zap233
Technically taking cuttings is also cloning, just generally slower.


[4/13/2026 9:58 PM] randy_om
cuttings are slower then cells?

{Reactions}
8_kitty_huh

[4/13/2026 10:25 PM] zap233
Traditional plant cuttings in ex-vitro conditions compared to sterile tissue culture.


[4/13/2026 10:26 PM] zap233
The primary advantage of plant tissue culture is that it's a faster method of cloning plants.


[4/13/2026 10:26 PM] randy_om
huh! i would just blindly think cuttings would be faster, i guess not


[4/13/2026 10:29 PM] zap233
With tissue culture, we get the advantages that the plant doesn't have anything to compete with in a sterile environment.  We are also able to feed it sugars directly, which reduces dependence on photosynthesis for speed of growth.  In addition, we can also force the plants in tissue culture to divide more rapidly by providing media with certain plant hormones added to it.


[4/13/2026 10:30 PM] zap233
The start up time is longer, but you can easily generate hundreds of thousands of plantlets.


[4/13/2026 10:31 PM] randy_om
Yeah, I see your points. I did think tissue culture would multiply faster. But I guess I originally thought the plant wouldn't have as much work to do if it was coming from a cutting.


[4/13/2026 10:31 PM] zap233

[4/20/2026 1:18 AM] huntrsaspoon
How much roughly does it cost for a starter setup to be able to do tissue culture?


[4/20/2026 3:26 AM] juzzy__
Budget $500, after you think you got it for $300 you’ll want to buy more stuff


[4/20/2026 4:13 AM] beer30247
It's like most hobbies, you can start out cheap or go crazy and spend $10k.  Starting out a clear tote, pressure canner, small scissors or exacto knife/scalpel, forceps, small scale, and syringes.  That's about it for "equipment".  Then it's on to ordering some type of nutrients, most use MS to start, pgrs (not needed to start), agar, 90% iso alcohol, and a spray bottle for the alcohol, bleach, and containers of some type.  The MS can be avoided too if you already grow hydroponically as you can just use the nutrients you already have on hand.  Agar does NOT need to be some special lab grade overpriced bs.  Food grade works just fine. 

I left out a tool sterilizer as open flame or heat in a sab isn't smart with all the alcohol fumes.  So either use bleach or just reset the whole box between cuttings if you really want to avoid contamination as much as possible.  Super slow yes, but doing stuff over and over because of contam is slower. 

Gonna guess starting with nothing at all $300 sounds about right.  What the pressure canner costs you is the biggest expense and variable.


[4/20/2026 12:47 PM] huntrsaspoon

[4/21/2026 3:02 PM] juzzy__
It takes about 2-3 hours to make a batch of media, depending on your setup. Then another hour or so to process a batch of explants - a batch being about 13 or so 5.5oz cups and cuttings. Being your new, you may need to add a bit more just because you’re going to be slower and make more mistakes.


[4/21/2026 3:04 PM] juzzy__

[4/21/2026 3:39 PM] zap233
It really depends on what the culture is doing.  If it's got air exchange or large enough that it's not running into gas issues, or drying out, popping the container open, or having exessive phenolic leaching, you can kind of just leave them.


[4/21/2026 3:39 PM] zap233
I've got some 16oz containers I haven't touched in 6 months and some B-caps jars I haven't touched in 4-5.


[4/21/2026 3:40 PM] zap233
I spend a good chunk of a day maybe twice a month.

[4/22/2026 4:20 AM] beer30247
The plant you are doing, it's all I do, needs more airflow than houseplants.  So venting whatever container you use isn't optional.  That's why media doesn't normally last a long time.  Now obviously the more you put in the container the longer it can last.  For just holding genetics 2 things.  One temp, as a grower I'm sure you know, growth slows down under like 70°F, use it to your advantage if you can.  Other thing is most commercial nutrients (MS, DKW, etc) all have one major flaw.  They stress the plant and cause browning over time.  So the solution for the time being is take them out of tc every say 6 months root them and grow them large enough to take nodes from and put back in tc.  I'm working on nutrients that don't cause that problem but obviously since it takes months for issues to show up it's a long process and I'm not the type to just be like "my stuff is the greatest ever plants can live forever" if I haven't tested it past a year.  I would say 90% of stuff online about tc for our plant is short term, all about growing nodes fast to make as many new plants in a small space.  Which is cool and all.  But for long term storage we still have a long way to go.

[4/30/2026 4:28 AM] beer30247
With as little light as most plants need in tc, until they run outa sucrose that is, one could likely just not bother lighting the shelf at all and just keep the room light on.


[4/30/2026 4:31 AM] beer30247
I have explants that I've left beside my flow hood for a couple of weeks that only get light from the room, nearest light is 4ft away and usually half covered with leaves.  So not much light ppfd wise.  They grow the same as in my tent with way more light.  But of course different plants different nutrient, sucrose, and pgr recipes may need different light levels.


[4/30/2026 4:41 AM] beer30247
The deeper one dives into what is really required to do tc the more one starts to understand why asia is able to dominate the market so well.  It doesn't take crazy amounts of money to startup.  You don't need some super crazy clean room, perfect environmental controls,a ton of expensive equipment, or expensive inputs.  It takes a flow hood, some measuring tools, and containers.  A way to clean tools, containers, and explants.  Water, something to make the liquid gel, something for the explant to eat, and labor.  Lots n lots of low skill labor.


[4/30/2026 6:05 PM] juzzy__
China dominates because their labor is cheap, their supplies are cheap and the .gov subsidized shipping costs dramatically. It's rediculous that I can buy $10 in widgets and it can ship from china to my door for "free" essentially. To reship those exact items would be more than $10 via USPS before you calculate the item's COGs

[5/25/2026 2:35 PM] ebo1744
Before buying a LFH check the weight -- I got on at auction, and it weighs enough it would be more than what the floor is expected to support.  BTW, the (international) uniform building code requires that a sleeping room floor be designed to support 30 pounds per square feet (psf), and I think my flow hood weighed something like 500# (which was a LOT more than 30psf).  So unless you are living/working on a concrete slab, be mindful of the weight, and think about weight spreaders for the feet/wheels.

{Reactions}
👆

Yeah I wondered why the massive bsc hoods were cheaper than manageable sized HLF hoods and found out first hand. The one I got was 1000lbs and it broke the elevator I used to move it. I paid more for shipping and help getting it moved into place than I did for the hood itself.

[5/28/2026 5:24 PM] zap233
Use a tote or other solid box.  The very old-school way is a glass aquarium on its side.  These soft vinyl sided containers do not work well since they flex and encourage too much air movement.


[5/28/2026 5:24 PM] zap233
My contamination rates dropped off dramatically when I stopped using a soft box.

[6/3/2026 9:11 AM] erwin_myco
You still can't measure such tiny amounts on a precision scale. Those still need to be measured out by making a stock solution. You can make smaller amounts of stock solution though, with the same precision.


[6/3/2026 2:01 PM] hienhc
do you mean using NaOH to dissolve the PGR?

[6/4/2026 8:44 AM] hienhc
how are you measuring with 10mg precision? big batches?


[6/4/2026 9:00 AM] erwin_myco
It's part of it. Just making like a 100ml of stock solution so you need to weight out 100mg to get a 1mg/ml solution. If you have a better, and calibrated scale you can make smaller amounts while still being accurate, so you don't have to through away old stock solutions to often etc. But measuring tiny milligram amounts on its own just won't be accurate, often also because tiny fractions can be lost during transfer etc

Use food service delis. They come in 8, 12, 16, 24, and 32oz.


[6/22/2026 3:53 PM] grilledcheeze_
Is there a reason other than price that you recommend the PP food service containers? Do you ever re-use the deli containers?


[6/22/2026 3:54 PM] chefdecuisinart
I'm a chef and they're readily available. I always have 100+ of each size on hand.


[6/22/2026 3:55 PM] chefdecuisinart
And yeah, they can be reused dozens/hundreds of times


[6/22/2026 3:57 PM] grilledcheeze_

[6/22/2026 3:58 PM] chefdecuisinart
I don't think there are any tradeoffs. Many smaller TC operations (such as Carnivero) use delis exclusively


[6/22/2026 3:58 PM] chefdecuisinart
I've bought cultures from them well over a year old, so they're definitely good for storage


[6/22/2026 4:00 PM] grilledcheeze_

[6/22/2026 4:08 PM] zap233
If your goal is archiving, I recommend avoiding vents, since it will cause the media to start drying up after several months.

[7/6/2026 8:25 PM] alessandra03468
I wanted to bring up something I've been running into during the sterilization step. Every time I take the culture medium containers out of the autoclave, right after the cycle finishes, I notice water droplets that have condensed on the underside of the lid and also on the inner walls of the container.

The issue is that this condensed water doesn't stay on the lid — it eventually drips back down into the container, adding moisture on top of what the medium already has. This creates a more humid environment inside the vessel than intended, and I suspect that extra humidity could be part of what's making it easier for fungi and bacteria to establish and spread once any contaminant is present (or gets introduced).

So far I haven't found a reliable way to get rid of this condensation without risking the sterility of the medium. Opening the container to wipe it down obviously isn't an option once it's sterile, so I'm a bit stuck on how to approach this.

One idea I had was letting the containers cool down slightly with the lid partially ajar while still inside the laminar flow hood, so any moisture could evaporate before sealing them completely — but I'm honestly not sure if that's a safe or effective approach, or if it introduces other risks.

Has anyone else dealt with this same problem? If so, how do you usually manage lid condensation after autoclaving without compromising sterility? Any tips, tricks, or experiences would be really appreciated


[7/6/2026 8:32 PM] urbanorganisms
One easy solution is to autoclave media in a bottle and the containers separately, and then pour under a flow hood. This way the agar sets with the containers open and no condensation is created in the process. 

Regardless though, condensation often forms later on, but condensation alone doesn’t cause contamination so it’s not like eliminating one will eliminate the other.


[7/6/2026 8:39 PM] zap233
All tissue culture flasks are going to be nearly 100% humidity since they're a sealed container with water.  It's normal, as is the condensation.  Some people pour it off in a sterile environment but it doesn't really affect anything so long as your explants/seeds aren't completely submerged.

[7/6/2026 9:02 PM] plantsinjars
You can drill holes through your container lids and add mycology filter patches. This helps with condensation and also allows for gas exchange. Phytotech also makes good glass jars (GA7 I think? GA-something) that allow for some gas exchange, although you need to keep your culture room/area very clean if you're going to use them

[7/16/2026 3:08 PM] ekatsim01

[7/16/2026 3:45 PM] creamyc00kie
I wonder also if my room itself would help. I have an extremely dry room for some reason. My house also doesn't have any mold problems like my old house does. Do you think a still airbox with the holes cut out would be better?


[7/16/2026 4:08 PM] ekatsim01
it might be easier for you to work in. The still air box itself only has two functions: 
1. Keeping the air from moving, thus reducing the likelihood of contamination. 
2. Acting as an isolation bubble. 

Beyond that, it is a matter of how well you can work with it, while maintaining sterile technique.


[7/16/2026 4:10 PM] creamyc00kie
Ohhh ok I see


[7/16/2026 4:13 PM] ekatsim01
One person just used an upside down tub, reaching up, under it.

Also, I did my first culture in the open air.
Five of twenty-five were uncontaminated.


[7/16/2026 4:16 PM] ekatsim01
not great odds, but it worked to a degree.


[7/17/2026 6:28 PM] creamyc00kie
Oh wow I would not expect some of them to just stay fine


[7/17/2026 6:35 PM] ekatsim01

Mold spores are everywhere though.
A few of the ones that did not make it actually grew bacteria from inside the plant though, so if my cleaning had been more effective, it would have been more successful.


[7/17/2026 6:38 PM] ekatsim01

the only contamination I am getting lately is from not quite getting the plants clean enough.


[7/17/2026 6:39 PM] ekatsim01

So all Amazon returners get thrown out regardless if you opened the package or not in most cases. 

There’s a market of people that buy the returns from Amazon(for pennies on the dollar) and resell those items. Most items are in perfect condition, sometimes people returned the item because it actually is defective and that is the bet resellers make.

This also happens for Home Depot, Lowell’s, grills and refrigerators etc

[8/16/2026 2:45 PM] grumpyalison
If one were to make a broke boy fan filter unit (box fan with filters duct taped on), is there a reason you couldn't
a) use it with a hood in front
b) put it on top of the hood so the fan is blowing from the top like most commercial fan filter units ive seen? 

I'll probably test both ways just because I have some very old begonia media to sacrifice, but I was curious if anyone had experience with either before


[8/16/2026 6:03 PM] urbanorganisms
No to both of your questions. However a box fan with a filter taped on will have way too strong of airflow which won’t be laminar. Not saying it can’t work/help, but it’s not ideal. Also, generally a horizontal flow hood is preferable to a vertical one, so if anything keep it horizontal. Both can work of course though


[8/17/2026 6:51 PM] grumpyalison
Fair enough. I was going for a fan filter unit over laminar flow hood as a test since it seems lots of people have good luck with them. Thanks for the thoughts!

{Reactions}

[9/10/2026 9:49 AM] jade.sec
Asking in this channel too for more exposure. Beginner Question alert! Are "Cool/white light" vs "warm white" different in terms of impact on tissue culture growth? 🙂 I just find warm white much prettier than pure white light but wanted to know any trade offs
Thanks! 🙂


[9/10/2026 3:29 PM] zap233
Fortunately, tissue culture plants don't require much light, as they get most of their energy from the sugar in the media.  Light type shouldn't matter much here.  Even ex vitro, it's more about saving a little bit of energy cost more than anything.


[9/10/2026 3:44 PM] juzzy__
Zap's correct but to answer your question, "vegetative" state plants do better under cool lights. I run all cool lights on my shelves


[9/10/2026 3:44 PM] juzzy__
Or get "full spectrum grow lights" if the price is close enough

Does everyone mostly use Karat cups in here? I’m trying to see if it’s better to have a bigger diameter container or no

I feel like I use more media when using a bigger container but it’s a problem of space not cost


[11/6/2026 6:25 PM] zap233
I prefer PhytoCap/B-cap jars because of the clarity, size, reusability, and slight air exchange.  Oh, and they can be autoclaved closed.  But they're $$$


[11/6/2026 6:26 PM] zap233
For the cups I think most people use either 5.5 or 16oz, but the 16oz are pretty big.

[11/9/2026 3:33 AM] z_thomason
Are you talking about for invitro or acclimation? Invitro don’t waste your money on grow lights just get some cheap shop lights. Plants invitro do not need a ton of light. They get most of their energy from the sucrose in the media. If you’re talking about after you pull them out of tc then yeah barrina is the way id go.
